About Argonne National Laboratory
Argonne is a U.S. Department of Energy laboratory managed by UChicago Argonne, LLC
under contract DE-AC02-06CH11357. The Laboratory’s main facility is outside Chicago, at
9700 South Cass Avenue, Argonne, Illinois 60439. For information about Argonne
and its pioneering science and technology programs, see www.anl.gov.

DOCUMENT AVAILABILITY
 Online Access: U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) reports produced after 1991 and a
 growing number of pre-1991 documents are available free at OSTI.GOV
 (http://www.osti.gov/), a service of the US Dept. of Energy’s Office of Scientific and
 Technical Information.

Reports not in digital format may be purchased by the public from the
National Technical Information Service (NTIS):
  U.S. Department of Commerce
  National Technical Information Service

  Alexandria, VA 22312

  Fax: (703) 605-6900
  Email: orders@ntis.gov

Reports not in digital format are available to DOE and DOE contractors from the
Office of Scientific and Technical

 US Efforts to Support Examinations at Fukushima –
 Expert Panel Meeting

 Damian Peko, Program Manager, U.S. Department of Energy
                                                   November 13, 2025

 Overview

 Forensics Effort Offers US Perspective to Fukushima Daiichi
 Examination Activities
 Objectives:
 • Develop consensus US input for high priority time-sequenced examination tasks and supporting research that
 can be completed with minimal disruption of TEPCO Decommissioning and Decontamination (D&D) activities
 • Evaluate obtained information to:
  Gain a better understanding of events that occurred in each unit at Daiichi
  Gain insights to reduce uncertainties in predicting phenomena and equipment performance
 during severe accidents
  Provide insights beneficial to TEPCO Phase 2 Fuel Debris Retrieval Evaluations
  Confirm/improve guidance for severe accident prevention, mitigation, and emergency planning
  Periodically update/refine original information requests
 • Facilitate implementation of Japan-led international research efforts to support D&D
 Motivations:
 • Provides US access to full-scale, prototypic data from multiple units with distinct
 accident signatures
 • Provides Japan access to US expertise in plant operations, severe accident modeling &
 Images Courtesy of ANS
 testing, and defueling & cleanup

$1/                                                       &

 Overview

 US Efforts Coordinated with Japan’s Decommissioning Roadmap

 Report documenting September 2024 DOE Forensics Expert
 Panel Meeting publicly available (See
                                                                                   https://doi.org/10.2172/2570504)

 Impact

 US Benefits from Forensics Effort
Benefits Gained:
• Information to ensure continued safe and economic operation of the existing fleet and proposed new advanced
  designs (LWR and non-LWRs):
   Post-Fukushima actions (SSC upgrades, FLEX and updated guidance based on Daiichi forensics Information) allowed
 continued operation of US plants, address challenges associated with correlated external, multi-unit events..
   Awareness of international efforts to address issues observed at Daiichi (e.g., hydrogen production and accumulation monitors,
 water level and temperature instrumentation, changes in operator guidance, testing to explain unresolved issues, etc.).
   NEW severe accident insights (e.g., in-vessel heatup and relocation, ex-vessel accident progression, SSC performance,
 combustible gas generation, etc.)
Anticipated Future Benefits:
• Full-scale BWR accident progression information to address many technology-neutral knowledge gaps in:
   RPV failure modes (impacts of dry-core scenarios, lower head penetrations, etc.)
   Melt relocation and interaction dynamics (melt/water interactions, corium concrete interactions)
   Combustible gas generation and transport dynamics
   PCV failure and leakage paths
   Fission product behavior (release, retention, and transport)
• Plant operational benefits (increasing flexibility, reducing personnel exposure, reducing costs) associated with 1F
  examination insights and new D&D technologies

                                                                &                                                          $1/

 Closing Remarks

 • CHALLENGES
 • INFORMATION RELEASE

$1/                  &

&'2()RUHQVLFV0HHWLQJ2YHUYLHZ

 US Efforts to Support Examinations at Fukushima Daiichi –
 Meeting Overview

 Joy Rempe,
 Technical Lead, DOE Forensics Support

                                                 November 13, 2025

 Daiichi Highlights since September 2024 Meeting

  • Contaminated water generation, treated water release and tank removal:
  Fifteen discharges successfully completed (16th discharge started on October 30, 2025)
  J9 area tank removal (12 welded tanks) completed in September 2025; preparations for J8 area tank removal underway
  Contaminated water generation reduced from ~540 m3/day in May 2014 to ~ 80 m3/day in FY2023 (surpassed original
      JRDORIP3/day by FY2025; new goal is 50-70 m3/day by FY2028)
  • SFP fuel removal:
  All fuel assemblies removed from 1F3 and 1F4 pools
  Preparations for 1F1 and 1F2 fuel removal continue (1F1 cover installation, 1F2 boom crane installation preparation, etc.)
  • 1F2 sample removal and initial examination results:
  1st 1F2 trial sample retrieval completed November 7, 2024. Sample transported to JAEA for initial examinations.
  2nd 1F2 sample retrieval completed April 23, 2025. Sample transported to JAEA for initial examinations.
  • Future examinations and expanded debris removal:
  1F3 drone investigation preparation initiated
  1F3 debris removal planning initiated

                                                              &                                                       $1/

  

 Status of September 2024 Meeting Recommendations / Actions

  • U.S. organizations should continue to provide Japanese organizations helpful information
  Additional IC operational insights provided in May 2025 report Section 2.3 and Appendix D.3 - completed
  Appendix B.2 Updates on clarifying details to support information requests (ex-vessel debris characterization information for MCCI insights,
 debris coolability, future RPV failure investigations, and additional D&D applications for new technologies (e.g., drones, robots, etc.) - completed
  • Additional analysis of available data should be performed to provide a technical basis for increasing the LWR RCS cooldown rate
 limit for off-normal events and normal operations (results could enhance safety, reduce operating costs and increased plant
 capacity factors of existing and proposed new reactors) – FY26 DOE LWRS program funding allocated
  • Additional evaluations should be completed to discern appropriate decay heat correlation for EPU applications – EPRI
 evaluations underway
  • An updated MELCOR/MAAP systems analysis ‘crosswalk’ comparison activity should be performed; it should consider: (a)
 LEISAN testing and 1F images; (b) comparisons of instrumentation readings versus actual plant conditions; (c) key MELCOR
 and MAAP input parameters and associated uncertainty distributions, and (d) extended time periods – delayed; but EPRI plans
 to start in 2026.
  • Benefits from 1F examinations and associated activities should be publicized, highlighting insights from Fukushima forensics
 information and associated R&D results (e.g., reducing severe accident knowledge gaps, changes to systems analysis code
 models, etc.)
  Panel session at ANS November 2025 meeting - completed
  BWROG BDBE Workshop – completed

 November 2025 Meeting Topics
  • Introductory Remarks – Nudi (EPRI) and Peko (DOE)
  • NDF - Yamanaka
  • TEPCO
  Recent Findings from Fukushima Daiichi Decommissioning Activities– Mizokami
  Update on Mid-to-Long Term Plan- Omura
  • NRA Investigation of Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Accidents (2024-2025) – Miyamoto
  • JAEA - Okamoto
  • Topic Area Discussions
  Area 1 – Gabor and Robb
  Area 3 – Farmer and Plys
  Area 4 – Luangdilok and Luxat
  Area 5 – Bunt and Williamson
  • Related EPRI Activities – Nudi
  • Related NRC-sponsored Activities – Luxat

 * See agenda for presentation schedule. Link to Meeting Agenda, Viewgraphs, September 2024 Meeting Report: Meeting Documents

 Current Status and Future Challenges
 of Fuel Debris Retrieval

                                 November 13, 2025
 Nuclear Damage Compensation and
 Decommissioning Facilitation Corporation (NDF)
 Yasunori YAMANAKA

 1XFOHDU'DPDJH&RPSHQVDWLRQDQG'HFRPPLVVLRQLQJ)DFLOLWDWLRQ&RUSRUDWLRQ 

 I. Introduction

$1/                                           &

                          

Understanding of the Current Status at 䠍F 

 䘟 14 years after the accident, the site is in a relatively stable state.
 • Steady progress has been made in efforts to reduce risks at Fukushima Daiichi

 Decommissioning of Fukushima Daiichi is progressing
 䘟 toward the phase of full-scale fuel debris retrieval.
 • Trial fuel debris retrieval conducted twice, moving into the Phase 3 in the Mid-
 and-Long-term Roadmap.
 • Although many difficulties to be resolved still exists, engineering study for full-
 scale retrieval is also underway.

 䘟 Work safety will become even more crucial in the future.
 • During the first trial retrieval, multiple operational problems occurred, and
 workers’ radiation doses also increased.
 • Ensuring "work safety" is the Primary prerequisite for future efforts.
 9 Larger-scale retrieval and longer work periods lie ahead.
 9 Flexible responses are essential at the site with a high degree of uncertainty.

 It is important to proceed with fuel debris retrieval step by step.
 9 It is difficult to plan large-scale engineering projects from the outset when significant
 uncertainties exist at the site.
 9 Start small and gradually expand the scale while gaining knowledge and lessons learned.

 • Conditions inside the reactor and buidling

 Key considerations for Step-by-Step Approach

 (1) Trial and error, and rework may occur at each stage.
 z Review designs and work procedures through trial and error
 z Expand the scale of retrieval while accumulating these experiences

 (2) Ensuring work safety is the primary prerequisite.
 z Starting work within a scope where safety can be ensured and then
 expanding that scope is a prerequisite for long-term sustainable
 decommissioning work.
 z Establishing a realistic path to risk reduction will lead to ensuring work
 safety.

 Required approach to future decommissioning work:
 (1) Integrated and timely decision-making at the site
 9 Make decisions based on a holistic understanding of diverse on-site information and
 perspectives
 9 Adjust directions in timely and appropriately according to on-site conditions and new
 findings
 9 Support regulatory decision through smooth information sharing and communication

 (2) Prioritization and strategic resource allocation
 9 Resource allocation based on a long-term perspective and on-site situations
 9 Consistency of priorities between whole and its parts (avoiding silos)

 With safety being ensured as the highest priority,
 steady and flexible progress needs to be made with
 on-site work involving significant uncertainties..

 1XFOHDU'DPDJH&RPSHQVDWLRQDQG'HFRPPLVVLRQLQJ)DFLOLWDWLRQ&RUSRUDWLRQ 

 III. Summary

                                             &                                               $1/

 7RZDUGV6DIHDQG5HOLDEOH)XHO'HEULV5HWULHYDO 

 䘟 Efforts are currently underway toward fuel debris retrieval.
 • Two trial retrievals were conducted, and analysis of these debris are underway.
 • Conceptual studies of large-scale retrieval methods are also underway.

 However, various difficulties and challenges may continue to arise in
 䘟 the complex and long-term decommissioning of Fukushima Daiichi.
 • As work progresses, unexpected challenges and difficulties may arise.
 • Consider not only individual technical issues but also overall approach to
 decommissioning work

 In particular, the key issue is how to proceed safely and steadily with
 䘟 the decommissioning work while dealing with uncertainties.
 • Without work safety, it is impossible to steadily progress with long-term work.
 • Learning from findings and lessons from actual work and incorporating flexibly
 into operations and plans will contribute to improving safety.

 7RZDUGV6DIHDQG5HOLDEOH)XHO'HEULV5HWULHYDO 

 Key Important Points for Future Endeavors

 (1) Step-by-Step Approach
 z Start work within a scope where safety can be ensured, and then
 expand that scope
 z Accept trial and error, and temporary rework as necessary steps.

 (2) Enhancing Environmental Improvement
 z Important foundational work affecting worker safety, retrieval methods
 and schedules

 (3) Overall Approach to Future Decommissioning Work
 z Integrated and timely decision-making
 z Prioritization and strategic resource allocation

1. Investigation of Unit 1 Isolation Condenser

 Investigation inside of the Unit 1 reactor building
                                                                                               (January 2025)

1. Investigation of Unit 1 Isolation Condenser(1/7)

 Purpose
  It has not yet been clarified how effective IC was in cooling the reactor during the accident. Therefore,
 by clarifying in detail the behavior of this equipment during the accident, lessons related to IC can be
 learned.

 Main points of investigation
 Confirmation of IC operation and system configuration in the event of an accident
 • The operation of IC and plant status in chronological order
 • The parameters related to IC (return thermometer and tank water level)
 Confirmation of IC equipment configuration through on-site investigation
 • Valve and return thermometer installation locations
 Investigation of non-condensable gas venting
 • Warming line system configuration, dose measurement, etc.
 Consideration of the need for regular testing under actual conditions

 A On-site investigation

 the return thermometer may have indicated approximately 140 C due to the inflow of
 steam, rather than heat conduction from the reactor.
 It is believed that the isolation valves (1A and 4A) inside the containment vessel
 IC (A) IC (B) remained open.

 1. Investigation of Unit 1 Isolation Condenser(6/7)
 The necessity of regular testing under actual conditions and the possibility of using it in the event of an accident
 Status of regular tests, etc.
 Only valve opening and closing tests were conducted on the isolation valves (3A, 3B) outside the containment vessel and the air-operated valves
 (17A, 17B, 20A, 20B) installed on the warming line.
 Operation tests under actual conditions using reactor steam or substitute steam were not conducted.
 Since there was no equipment installed to simulate the operation of IC, it was difficult to confirm and understand the plant's behavior in advance
 when IC was activated or deactivated.
 Test valve IC behavior (until the tsunami arrives) and issues
 To Main Steam Line Upon receiving a high reactor pressure signal, IC systems (A) and (B) were automatically
 activated to depressurize the reactor from approximately 7 MPa to about 4 MPa within around
 10 minutes. Thereafter, IC(A) maintained reactor pressure at approximately 6 to 7 MPa.
 Reactor steam flow
 After the reactor scram, when IC was activated, the actual tank water temperature had not yet
 reached 100 C, so it is unlikely that steam generation sounds occurred immediately afterward.
                          IC(A)                                                     IC(B)
 (System (A) is assessed to reach 100 C around the time the tsunami arrived.)
 R Due to the lack of operational experience under actual condition, several issues
 were identified, including recognition of steam generation sounds, methods for
 P confirming steam generation, and understanding the operable time (approximately 10
 V hours).
 Possibility of utilizing IC in the event of a severe accident
 IC should be configured to return cooling water to the reactor pressure vessel without
 reducing the reactor's cooling water supply.
 Simulation-based training should be conducted to verify whether IC can achieve both
 hot and cold shutdown during emergencies.

 1. Investigation of Unit 1 Isolation Condenser(7/7)
 Summary
 A

 The open/closed state of the isolation valves (1A and 4A) inside the containment vessel
 remains unknown. However, even after the reactor core was damaged, the water level in
 the tank decreased, so it is estimated that the valves remained open enough to partially
 restore the function of the IC system.
 Since IC is passive equipment and does not require power like active equipment, its
 function may temporarily recover due to changes in plant conditions (e.g., steam
 generation), provided the system configuration is properly established.
 The possibility of non-condensable gas being sent through the warming line is currently
 difficult to determine.
 To better understand IC’s function and overall plant behavior, it is important to conduct
 operational tests under actual conditions and to have simulation facilities in place.

Conduct chemical analysis of the thermal effects of hardened cement (simulated specimen) Phase (2024)
Concrete heating test results
 Concrete and reinforcing bars lose strength due to high temperatures
 (Fig.1).
 The concrete is thought to have suffered damage due to expansion
 cracks occurring in both the hardened cement and aggregate due to
 the high temperature (Fig.2).
 Hardened cement remains stable or metastable at room temperature
 Figure 1. Decrease in thermal strength of concrete

 structural phase transition occurs -such as from ȕ-C2S (dicalcium Structural phase transition

 to have caused powdering (the dusting phenomenon).

2. Analysis of Concrete damage to Unit 1 reactor containment vessel pedestal (5/5)

Summary

 The hardened cement (on the pedestal sidewall) remains stable or metastable at
 room temperature or under constant heating up to high temperatures; however,
 when subjected to a gradual cooling process from high temperatures, a structural
 phase transition occurs, leading to volume expansion, which is highly likely to
 have resulted in powdering (the dusting phenomenon). (Possible damage in low-
 temperature environments)

 Phase IV will be carried out, including Preliminary analysis.

3. Clarification of Unit 3 hydrogen explosion phenomena (2/4)
Confirmation of hydrogen behavior inside the Unit 3 (CIGMA experiments at JAEA )
Key Issue
When the release of steam-hydrogen started (simulating the actual situation in a reactor building),
the walls were likely at room temperature. flammable
Thus, it is necessary to verify whether the phenomenon—where a flammable atmosphere tends atmosphere
to form more readily in regions distant from the injection point upon the release of hydrogen and
steam—also occurs under conditions in which the wall temperature gradually increases.

 Result
Continued CIGMA experiments at JAEA have consistently demonstrated that even with gradually
increasing wall temperatures, the gas mixture remains combustible for a longer duration in areas Figure 1. Experimental results without wall
 temperature control

3. Clarification of Unit 3 hydrogen explosion phenomena (3/4)
Confirmation of the pressure generated in the event of a hydrogen explosion inside the Unit 3 (Nagaoka University of Technology)
 Cracks caused
 Key Issue by a hydrogen
 explosion
 During an actual accident, the gas that leaked from the reactor
 containment vessel into the reactor building is presumed to have
 contained not only hydrogen and other combustible gases but also
 inert gases such as steam. Therefore, it is essential to
 characterize the pressure behavior under gas composition
 conditions that closely replicate those of a real accident scenario.
 Figure 3. Damaged main beam in the 3rd floor ceiling

 observed in Unit 3 (e.g., beam breakage) could occur.(Fig.4). (nitrogen)

                                                                        &                                                                               $1/

  3. Clarification of Unit 3 hydrogen explosion phenomena (4/4)
  Summary
 Hydrogen Behavior: CIGMA experiments
 Under conditions where the wall temperature was not controlled, it confirmed that when a
 mixed gas of hydrogen (simulated with helium) and steam is released into a multi-tiered
 structure simulating the reactor building, combustible atmospheres with high
 concentrations of air and hydrogen can form and persist for longer durations in areas
 away from the gas release point.
 It was also confirmed that even under conditions similar to those of an actual reactor
 building (conditions with large heat capacity), the explosive conditions lasted longer in
 spaces further from the release point.

 Hydrogen Explosion Pressure: Combustion tests with hydrogen, methane (as a
 combustible organic gas), and inert gases (nitrogen or steam)
 Experiments confirmed that even under conditions with inert gases that typically
 suppress combustion, explosions strong enough to cause damage observed in Unit 3
 (e.g., beam breakage) could occur.

                                                                                                 &                                                                                         $1/

  4. Clarification of Unit 1 shield plug displacement and deformation (3/3)
  Summary

 The most plausible hypothesis is that the event was caused by pressure resulting
 from a hydrogen explosion in the reactor well (Hypothesis 4).

 However, the reactor well—where the shield plug is installed—is not airtight.
 Therefore, we examined the possibility of a hydrogen explosion when hydrogen gas
 entering the reactor well is purged within a short time. Despite uncertainties in the
 assumptions, we confirmed that the condition is valid for a short duration of 1 to 4
 minutes.

5. Analysis of monitoring posts related to Unit 1 venting (2/2)
 Progress of the investigation
 A
 Measurements from the Unit 6 SGTS monitor (0.7 km
 from Unit 1) and the Koriyama MP (3.0 km from Unit 1)
 showed peak dose rates coinciding with the Unit 1 vent.
 1 minute after release Fig.1 and Fig.2
 NHK will perform image analysis of this footage to
 determine the wind speed and direction at the time of
 the release.
 3 minute after release JAEA will utilize the wind speed and direction data from
 NHK as input for its Local-scale High-resolution
 Atmospheric Dispersion and Dose Assessment System
 (LHDDAS).
 10 minute after release LHDDAS can assess the concentration and deposition
 of atmospheric radioactive materials within a few
 kilometers, as well as the resulting radiation dose
 distribution, which will then be compared with measured
 MP data.

  Isotopic distribution of uranium exists within the reactor core (about 1% to 4%).
  Even in fresh fuel, vertical and horizontal variations are present

  Differences in fuel replacement cycles also affect the distribution.
  Loading pattern of the ²³຿U/total U ratio in Unit 2’s reactor core was created.

 distribution pattern remains.

Even from the axial distribution, both the caluculated ²³຿U enrichment and plutonium concentrations
show significant variation. By assuming local melting, it is unlikely to coincide with the core average.

 3UHYLRXV(VWLPDWLRQVRI)XHO'HEULVRQWKH3HGHVWDO)ORRU

  Initially, it was assumed that the temperature of the fuel debris was not very high, based
 on the observation that structural components such as the CRD exchanger frame and
 cable trays showed no significant deformation or damage.
  This led to the hypothesis that the debris did not reach temperatures sufficient to cause
 melting or thermal deformation, and that the relatively low temperature was due to a high
 content of structural materials.
  In such a case, the uranium content would be around 10 wt%, which can be solid-
 solutioned into iron-based oxides.
  A scenario was proposed in which molten iron-based oxides with low melting points
 migrated through porous media composed of uranium oxides, eventually damaging the
 RPV and falling into the pedestal.

 Considerations Based on the Analysis Results of the Fuel Debris
  from the First Trial Retrieval:
  The results of isotopic analysis suggest that uranium may be
 relatively well mixed.
  Thermodynamic calculations indicate that if the sample solidified
 from a molten state, the phases observed in fracture
 and cross-sectional surfaces can be explained.
 Future Outlook:
  As this is only one data point, it is important to continue accumulating data.
  The second fuel debris sample is scheduled for detailed analysis in near future.
  Compared to the first sample, the second one appears to be more fragmented
 in appearance, suggesting that there may be differences in the formation
 mechanisms.

 Using the thermo-fluid-structure coupling analysis, estimate the location and
 timing of lower head failure

 the creep damage technique. (Effects of the heat source location caused by the material accumulation
 and characteristics)

damage is likely to occur at penetrations such as the control rod guide tubes and stub tubes

 CIGMA experiments

 GOTHIC analysis CIGMA analysis Initial and boundary conditions
 Total Vol. у 50 m
 3

 *helium as a hydrogen substitute

  CIGMA containment vessel is used to mimic the reactor building (R/B).
  Partition plates are installed inside the CIGMA vessel to simulate the hierarchical structures of R/B.
  Gas (steam + helium*) is injected at the top of the CIGMA apparatus to simulate the leakage via shield plug.
  The upper half is externally cooled to condense steam on the vessel wall surface.
  Volume scaling is applied to simulate the hydrogen transport in 1F3.
  Focuses on non-condensable gas transport and accumulation driven by advection and condensation.

July 2025 TEPCO Unit 3 retrievals plan
  o TEPCO provided a public plan for Unit 3 remediation dated July 24, 2025,
Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 entitled “Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Power Station Unit 3 Design
 Deliberation for Fuel Debris Retrieval Method.”
  o The pertinent high-level information from the presentation is copied on
 my next page.
  o The TEPCO focus is on Unit 3 remediation, so we must ask ourselves:
 • What information do we have that could specifically help with Unit 3
 remediation, and what knowledge gaps might exist?

 Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

 Potential value of Fukushima debris sorting
 o Our motivation for this presentation is the potential value of Fukushima
 Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 debris sorting: High fuel content vs. Low fuel content debris.
 • Packaging requirements: Shielding, weight, movement, cost and schedule.
 • Interim storage requirements: On-site, near-site.
 • Ultimate disposal requirements: Location and cost would depend on fuel
 content.
 • Can an interim storage package be used with little or no modification for
 ultimate storage?
 • The “threshold” for high fuel vs low fuel will be the key factor in technical
 choices, regulatory approval, and public acceptance.
 • “Zero Fuel” is not a threshold option.
 o Even a minor percentage of lower cost packages & disposal options can
 vastly improve the financial ability to retrieve Fukushima debris.

 5

 Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

 Application of AI/MV to debris sorting
 o Automated sorting is an industrial standard, and these days machine
 Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 vision (MV) is often used to assist.
 o Sorting can simply mean “accept” versus “reject”, which is the most basic
 function we could ask for: “Non-negligible fuel content” versus “Negligible
 fuel content”, with a threshold to be determined.
 o We successfully piloted MV in 2000 to identify and count pieces of Hanford
 scrap and to estimate reactive surface area (uranium metal).
 o We can use MV to easily distinguish undamaged components from fuel
 pellets – proven this year with simulants at FAI.
 o We can extend this to distinguish components embedded in, or encrusted
 with, formerly molten fuel.

 6

$1/                                             &

Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

Application of AI/MV to debris sorting
  o Sorting becomes the hardest for formerly molten debris, so we need to
Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 apply further information.
 • Density can distinguish metal & its oxides from fuel, but internal porosity of
 formerly molten material adds fuzziness.
 • We have expectations from models and experiments that we can exploit the
 potential for debris stratification.
 • In-vessel corium versus ex-vessel corium: The more concrete slag that is
 added, the less we can use density, and the more likely we have no
 stratification.
  o So, retrieving from upper versus lower layers of debris is an application.
  o The challenge is to use AI/MV with density and maybe dose measurements
 to facilitate even a minor amount of debris sorting.

 7

Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

How ROSAU debris can provide supporting information
  o ROSAU debris appears to be the ideal simulant to train
Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 an MV system to sort Fukushima debris.
  o We need detailed information on stratification,
 obtainable by XRF, to have a priori knowledge of
 composition versus elevation.
  o Then we need to cut pieces spanning the elevation and
 composition range.
  o Then a combination of pictures and density information
 can be used to train a system.
  o We need to also understand how the Unit 3 debris
 might be stratified or not: Where might this work?
  o How can we make this work: logistics, cost, etc.?
 Image Courtesy of ANL 8

 TOPIC 3 - CORE DEBRIS

 MODELING INSIGHTS BASED ON
 FORENSICS FINDINGS

 MITCH FARMER

 Reactor Safety Technology Expert Panel Forensics Meeting, November 13-14, 2025

 PRESENTATION OUTLINE

$1/                                                  &

 COMMENTS ON ANALYSIS OF FUEL DEBRIS
 SAMPLES INSIDE THE PEDESTAL OF 1F2 (1/3)

   Two small corium samples have been successfully retrieved from the pedestal
 region of 1F2; see locations below.
   These samples have been analyzed for radiological content, and the first sample
 has been analyzed for chemical composition/structure.
   Analyses of this type are important because they can provide insights into the
 accident progression/conditions, and as importantly, provide key information to
 support planning for D&D (i.e., guidance on tooling, removal techniques, …).

 First debris Second debris
   sample 0.693 g                                                                              sample 0.187 g

 Images courtesy of TEPCO, Holdings

 3

 COMMENTS ON ANALYSIS OF FUEL DEBRIS
 INSIDE THE PEDESTAL OF 1F2 (2/3)
 For the first sample (near pedestal wall), analysis has provided
  elemental composition (ICP/AES) and phase structure
  (SEM/EDS) (see data to right).
 Results indicate (U,Zr)O2-x with metallic (Fe/Ni) inclusions.
 – Expected for significant core damage events involving
 extensive melting of fuel and structures.
 Composition is consistent with SA code predictions.
 – Supports the idea that SA codes can be used to predict
 debris compositions to inform D&D.
 Image
 Metal phase composition is 66/29/5 wt% Fe/Cr/Ni, roughly courtesy of
 TEPCO,

 COMMENTS ON ANALYSIS OF FUEL DEBRIS
 INSIDE THE PEDESTAL OF 1F2 (3/3)
  Mass/volume measurements for first sample (below) indicate material density is ~ 6900
 kg/m3.
  Based on the measured composition, the theoretical (i.e. porosity-free) density is ~8200
 kg/m3.
 – This indicates ~16% sample porosity; consistent with values seen in numerous core
 debris samples from reactor materials tests.
  Sample density of ~6900 kg/m3 is rather high, even when accounting for porosity, indicating
 that the sample does not contain any appreciable amount of concrete decomposition
 products.
 – Consistent with chem. analyses.
 – However, this conclusion is limited to the upper surface debris layer sample.
 DISCLAIMER: As Shinya points out, these observations are based on a single corium
 sample, and more data are needed to confirm any observations.

 5
 Images courtesy of TEPCO, Holdings

 PEDESTAL WALL (1/9)
  2024 1F1 examinations by TEPCO revealed extensive
 damage to the PCV pedestal walls with exposed rebar
 and crust ledges/shelves found at elevations up to 1.5
 from the pedestal floor.
 – Elevations difficult to interpret/rationalize given
 estimated core debris depths in the pedestal/drywell
 regions following vessel failure. Image courtesy of TEPCO, Holdings

  NRA is examining possible mechanisms to explain these findings; i.e.,
 The concrete is thought to have suffered damage due to expansion cracks
 occurring in both the hardened cement and aggregate due to the high
 temperature. Hardened cement remains stable or metastable at room
 temperature or under constant heating up to high temperatures; however, when
 subjected to a gradual cooling cycle from a high temperature, a structural
 phase transition occurs causing volume expansion likely causing powdering
 (the dusting phenomenon).
  Utilizing this same basic concept, MCCI modeling improvements have been
 made to CORQUENCH in an attempt to reproduce some of the features of cavity
 ablation observed in 1F1 over 11 days of dry cavity ablation.
 6

$1/                                                &

PEDESTAL WALL (2/9)
 Evidence of ‘Upper Cavity Ablation’ (UCA) during dry MCCI has been provided
  by material MCCI tests; two examples shown below.
 Two mechanisms examined as possible contributors to UCA:
  1. Melt ‘foaming’, driven by sparging concrete decomposition gases, elevates the upper
 surface of the melt pool, leading to UCA by direct contact with melt.
  2. Radiation heat transfer from the upper surface of the debris to adjacent concrete walls
 leads to UCA.
 CCI-2 ablation profile: LCS concrete, 5 CCI-3 ablation profile: SIL concrete, 2.3
 hours dry cavity ablation hours dry cavity ablation

PEDESTAL WALL (3/9)
 Can melt foaming correlations explain UCA observed in reactor material tests?
 – Predictions improved, but application to 1F1 scenarios does not provide
 satisfactory results.
 CCI-2 (LCS) predictions of debris surface CCI-3 (SIL) predictions of debris surface

 PEDESTAL WALL (4/9)
  Given previous findings, a radiation heat
 transfer (RHT) driven UCA model was then
 implemented. Turned out to be technically
 difficult, involving a step-wise series of
 upgrades that were needed to bring the
 model into agreement with test data. Steps
 were as follows:

 1. Initial approach assumed RHT from debris upper surface to adjacent concrete
 sidewall; simple view factor for orthogonal flat plates utilized.
 – UC axially discretized to calculate localized behavior due to RHT.
 – First step: assumed that the surface temperature of decomposing concrete was
 the ablation temperature (~1500 K).
 – Subsequent steps had to add the feature to model ‘cavity wall powdering’ at a
 lower temperature for which concrete effectively looses all strength (~800 C =
 1073 K).

 9

 PEDESTAL WALL (5/9)
  Additional steps were required to bring the model into better compliance with test
 data:
 2. Improved the concrete ablation model to account for conduction heat transfer
 into the concrete. Helped reduce ablation, but not enough.
 3. Added a new view factor model that accounted for wall curvature on local RHT
 deposition. Better cavity shape, but still over-predicting UCA.
 4. Added a gray gas model to improve RHT transmittance predictions and reduce
 predicted ablation at upper elevations. Helped, but not enough.
 5. Upgraded the simple cavity geometry model (SCGM) with an improved cavity
 geometry model (ICGM) that discretized both the UC and melt zone regions to
 more accurately predict debris surface location.
 6. Finally added a RHT absorption model due to ‘soot’ (i.e., aerosols) to knock
 down predictions of ablation at high elevations (common feature in modeling of
 HT in boiler applications).
 – Utilized SURC and ACE/MCCI aerosol density measuments for a sanity
 check on expected aerosol densities during MCCI.

 STATUS OF THE REDUCTION OF SEVERE
 ACCIDENT UNCERTAINTIES (ROSAU) PROJECT
  ROSAU-I wrapped up in March 2025
  Knowledge gaps addressed: i) underwater melt Under water melt spreading facility
 spreading, and ii) impact of high metal content on
 spreading and coolability.
  Main project elements:
 1. Reactor material underwater melt spreading
 (MST) tests (5)
 2. Reactor material Debris Coolability and MCCI
 (DCAM) tests (5)
 3. Supporting model development and validation
  High level outcomes: i) wet spreading is distinctly
 different than dry spreading, and ii) melt composition DCAM facility
 (particularly unoxidized Zr) has a profound effect on
 cooling behavior, debris morphology, and H2 evolution. HX

  Substantial progress on spreading and coolability
 modeling; results can be incorporated into SA codes.
 CT RV
  Plans have been developed for a ROSAU-2 project; VT

 currently under discussion with potential sponsors.

 Overview of
 Industry
 Beyond
 Design Basis
 Workshop
 R A N DY B U N T A N D B I L L
 WILLIAMSON

 Workshop Focus
 Provide knowledge of industry beyond design basis events such that the participants internalize
 multiple aspects of the events versus the typical history summary. Personnel that responded or
 that were involved with aspects of the recovery from these events were involved in the
 development and in some cases the presentation of the materials for the workshop.

 The format of the workshop provided overview case studies of the conditions, events and plant
 personnel responses. Then an overview of the codes, standards, regulation and models
 developed from these events were reviewed. Followed by a discussion of the industry impact of
 the event.

 The workshop was broken down into sessions related to the reviewed events and was recorded for
 future development into reference or training modules.

                                                  &                                                 $1/

 

 Gap Workshop Addressed
 Basic knowledge of the significant beyond design basis events in the nuclear industry while frequently
 reviewed, does not provide the current nuclear worker the information to broaden the application of the
 information to apply to the industry and typically lacks linkage to the tools developed and informed from
 the events, recovery and fallout.

 The wealth of knowledge and similarity of these events have led to many industry tools (Codes,
 Regulation, INPO SOERs, MAPP, Gothic, US National Lab codes.

 First or second generation nuclear workers that lived through or responded to these events are at or
 near retirement and the changes in the industry post event by personal experience will soon be lost.

 Memorializing the deeper insights and learnings from the holistic view of these industry shaping events
 is valuable to current and future generations of nuclear workers.

 Events Covered
 1979 Browns Ferry Fire
 1979 Three Mile Island Loss of Cooling
 1986 Chernobyl Prompt Critcality
 1980’s Failure to scram:
            BFN3 (June 26, 1980)
            Salem (February 22 and 25, 1983)
 LaSalle Unit 2 Power Flow Oscillations (March 9, 1988)
 2011 Fukushima Loss of Electric Power/Infrastructure

 Investigation of E
 Ell-Wakil
 - from EPRI TBR for HBU Plants

3 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

 Decay Heat Uncertainties for Plants with Power Uprates
  Volume 2 of the EPRI SAMG Technical Basis Report
 proposes use of El-Wakil correlation as tool for simple
 decay heat estimates
 – Using form associated with infinite irradiation time
 – Originally compared to 1973 ANS-5.1 standard
  BWROG uses El-Wakil correlation in EPG/SAG
 guidelines and TSC guidelines
  Issue reported in 2024 Forensics meeting where
 extended power uprate (EPU) plants had to bias El-
 Wakil by factor of 1.3 to achieve reasonable decay
 heat estimates
  EPRI investigated this and compared to ANSI decay
 heat curves to evaluate a proposed solution for the
 TBR

4 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

$1/                                                                    &

 Comparison to ANSI 5.1-2005 Standard
  Two ANSI calculations performed
 – One for end of cycle (EOC)

 – One for beginning of cycle (BOC)

  Included +/- 2ʍ uncertainty relative to
 El-Wakil & El-Wakil with factor of 1.3
 correction
  Confirms findings that El-Wakil
 underpredicts decay heat estimates
 for typical accident management
 timeframe
 – El-Wakil with x1.3 correction
 reasonably matches ANSI at EOC, with
 some conservatism during this time
5 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

 Comparison to ANSI 5.1-2005 Standard (continued)
  When expanded out to 200
 hours
 – Nominal El-Wakil within ANSI
 uncertainty and approaches EOC
 values
 – Corrected El-Wakil is
 overestimating decay heat
  El-Wakil x 1.3 provides
 reasonable estimate of decay
 heat from ~5-minutes to 100
 hours after shutdown
6 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

                                                                    &                                     $1/

 Comparison to ANSI 5.1-2005 Standard (continued)

7 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

 Comparison to BFN Unit 3 Cycle 21 Midcycle Heatup
  Browns Ferry heatup data
 compared against El-Wakil and
 ANSI5.1 values
 – Review confirmed findings that TVA
 reported that El-Wakil
 underpredicted decay heat
 – El-Wakil with a 1.3x correction factor
 slightly overpredicted the observed
 heatup
  After review of inputs to ANSI5.1,
 not immediately apparent why
 this behavior would be associated
 with EPU
8 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

$1/                                                       &

 New correlation proposed for SAMG estimates
  Correlation proposed which is valid out
 to ~1500 hours after shutdown
  Produces more accurate estimate
 across wider range of applicability
 relative to both El-Wakil and El-Wakil
 w/correction
 – Minimal calculation burden

  EPRI currently awaiting comparison
 data against other industry EPU plants
 for additional benchmarking
 – Once consensus reached, EPRI to issue an
 errata for SAMG TBR documenting this
 new proposal for decay heat estimates

 Communication of Code Best Practices from 1F
 Evaluations
  Primary form of modeling best practices for MAAP
 communicated through sample MAAP parameter files
 (i.e., input decks) provided for each version
 – Model parameters introduced or have revised defaults to
 reflect the state of knowledge or expected range where
 uncertainty is expected to exist
  EPRI also communicates research findings through
 publications, white papers, and other deliverables
 – In 2025, EPRI released standalone white paper on
 application of the MAAP5 Zonal model developed post-
 Fukushima
 – Additionally, newly introduced MAAP Wiki logs all of the
 post-Fukushima response reports and model
 enhancement documents in single location for users as
 reference

1 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

 Parameter Uncertainty Models
  Probability distributions assigned to parameters based on
 what is believed to be their “correct” value
 – “Correct” meaning the value that leads to most accurate
 simulation of a particular event
 – Accuracy of probability distributions directly related to the state
 of knowledge for a given parameter
  Fukushima uncertainty assessments applied EPRI
 methodology to range of MAAP modeling parameters
 applicable to Mark I BWR designs
 – Approach designed to be extensible to all PWR and BWR
 modeling parameters in the MAAP code
 – These uncertainty distributions including expected mean values
 carried through to EPRI’s MAAP uncertainty analysis best
 practices report

2 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

$1/                                                                    &

 Best Practices and Expected Benefits when Applying
     MAAP5 Zonal Model (3002032034)
  Recent investigations into Fukushima Daiichi accident
 progression performed by various Japanese entities have
 demonstrated the importance of exercising the MAAP5 zonal
 model to capture the expected containment stratification
 behavior
  Zonal model introduced to add additional fidelity for pool
 liquid-phase stratification and gas-phase stratification in
 containment
  Focus of white paper to Best Practices and Expected Benefits when
 Applying MAAP5 Zonal model

 – Characterize modeling applications where MAAP users should
 consider exercising the Zonal model
 – Encourage broader use of the MAAP5 Zonal model within the
 MAAP User Group

 White paper published August 2025 - Link

 Mechanistic Pool Scrubbing Model
  Motivation for model development:
 – A concern was raised that the DF the decontamination factor calculated
 by MAAP for a sparger injection release geometry was not monotonically
 increasing as the mass fraction of steam increased
 DF calculated by MAAP
 Mass fraction of steam
 4 m pool 6 m pool
                            0.0                                      2,218                    561
                            0.5                                      106.7                   106.7
                            0.9                                       19.5                    19.5
                           0.99                                       25.6                    55.7

  A mechanistic model was developed in MAAP5 to be an additional
 option for pool scrubbing.
16 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

$1/                                                                 &

 Mechanistic Pool Scrubbing Model-Pool Scrubbing Zones
  Injection Zone:
 – The gas can leave the discharge location as bubbles or by forming a jet,
 depending on the discharge volume to flow area ratio.
  Condensation Zone
 – After the flow has developed the plume will rise and any steam will
 condense if conditions allow.
  Bubble Rise Zone
 – The mechanism for decontamination changes once the rising plume is in
 thermal equilibrium with the surrounding pool

 Incorporating UW Madison Bubble Rise Experimental Data into Pool
 Scrubbing Driver
 Impact of Bubble Size on Scrubbing
 Mechanisms:
  Variations in bubble size influence
 the efficiency of scrubbing
 mechanisms, including Brownian
 diffusion and inertial deposition.
  The effects are particularly notable
 for extreme aerosol particle sizes,
 highlighting the interplay between
 bubble properties and particle
 behavior
 Kyle F. Becker, Experimental Measurements Of Fission Product Retention Via Bubble Transport In A Water Coolant
 Pool, University Of Wisconsin – Madison, 2020.

 Mechanistic Pool Scrubbing Model – Bubble Rise DF

  Observed Trends Across Benchmarks
 – As submergence increases, the DF increases significantly across all
 aerosol median diameters (AMMDs)
 – Deeper submergence increases aerosol capture efficiency by increasing
 the residence time of bubbles
 – Larger particles are more effectively removed even at lower
 submergences, while smaller particles rely more on bubble interactions

 25 © 2025 Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. All rights reserved.

 Incorporating CRIEPI Experimental Data into MAAP
 Benchmarking – Jet Injection

  DFJET = exp(F(d) * vg)
 – DFJET increases exponentially with
 gas velocity
 – DFJET increases with particle
 diameter
  Large transition between bubble
 and jetting at Weber << ͳͲହ

 Comparison for Example Sequences
  Comparison of old (SUPRA-based) and new (mechanistic) pool
 scrubbing models using a development version of the MAAP6 code.
 – Cumulative DF Plotted
  BWR Mark I Short Term Station Blackout.
 – DC power was assumed to be lost immediately
 – SRVs were assumed to stick open on first lift
 – Fission Products discharged into subcooled pool
  BWR Mark I Long Term Station Blackout.
 – DC power credited 12 hours
 – SRVs stuck upon once temperature of gas reaches over 700 K
 – Fission Products discharged into saturated pool

 Evolving Se ve re Accide nt St a t e of Know le dge
 • Fukushima Daiichi Units 1, 2 and 3
 progressed to ex -vessel core damage
 • Three fundamentally different accidents
 • Unit 1 unmitigated
 • Units 2 and 3 partially mitigated
 • Three different damage end states
 • Ex-vessel core damage conditions most
 important contribution to knowledge base
 • Unit 1 has had late failure of containment floor
 despite no water injection for a few days?
 • Did debris spread like viscous lava?
 • Unit 2 has peripheral lower head failure?
 • ~80% of debris in lower plenum
 • Does debris material distribution provide any
 insight related to potential for material
 segregation?
 • Unit 3 has debris localized within reactor
 pedestal? 8SSHURSHQLQJ‫&ق‬5' UDLO‫ك‬
 73
 • Large solid debris mass under center of vessel

  

Mot iva t ion for Mode rnizing MELCOR
• MELCOR has traditionally been flexible code
 • Building block approach for CVH/FL, HS together with species transport in RN has enabled a
 broad range of facilities to be represented
• MELCOR capability requirements have been expanding
 • New types of core degradation phenomena for LWRs
 • Non -LWRs and other types of (innovative) water -moderated technologies
• Other aspects of the code have made it more difficult to evolve
 • COR representation has always been more rigid and in some sense restricted to traditional
 LWR core damage progression
 • Difficult or impossible to properly represent many types of reactor cores (e.g., prismatic heat pipe
 reactors, CANDU, etc.)
 • CVH and COR explicit coupling make code tricky to apply for SFRs and molten salt systems
 • CAV physics and numerics have struggled to adapt to advances in ex -vessel debris cooling
 • LHC has not been generally usable
 • Ex-vessel water ingress modeling stability challenges
• We cannot efficiently e va lu a te th e im p lica tio n s o f in sigh ts fro m Fu ku sh im a with a

The ore t ica l Forma lis m for Ris k-Informe d Mode ling
a nd Simula t ion – Comple xit y a nd Accide nt Ana lys is
• Equations encountered across MELCOR involve
  rate of change of some state with time
• Complexity of modeling arises because of
  dynamic manner in which states coupled
 • Prior to accident many interactions between
 “states” do not exist
 • For example, fuel mass cannot be transported to
 chemically interact with cladding
• Process of core degradation exhibits features
  of combinatorial evolution
 • With time, increasingly more degrees of freedom
 are liberated
• Current models for many processes can be
  reduced to canonical form

Ge ne ra lize d St ruct ure Re pre s e nt a t ion
• Development of 2D heat structure thermal modeling has been ongoing for a while
 • Overhauled this legacy effort to introduce a modernized, unstructured finite volume capability
 • No assumed dimensionality
• Typically focused on equations that represent heat conduction through structures
 • Capability being applied to generalized representation of species transport through structural materials
 • For example, radionuclide transport (interest in tritium transport in fusion applications as well as non -LWR
 applications)
• Structures can interface with core components represented as ”nodes” in the extensible
  network topology that allows us to introduce components a t will
 • Stru ctu re s ca n a lso in te rfa ce with o th e r stru ctu re s th ro u gh tra n sp o rt in te rfa ce s

Fa cilit a t ing Gre a t e r Explora t ion – Plug-ins (Troy
Ha s kin)
• Fundamental goal

 Pro vid e u se rs a m e a n s o f p re cise ly o ve rrid in g a n d a u gm e n tin g
 MELCOR’s co m p u ta tio n a l gra p h .

• Provide C-interface to build plug -in libraries
 • Libraries loaded at runtime
 • Provide as many libraries as needed
• Provide Database library
 • Allow access to MELCOR’s public runtime database
 • Allow setting plug -in data into public database for restart (and sharing with other plug -ins)
 • Provide CMake module for easy linking and compilation with MELCOR distribution
• Examples
 • Highly specialized models for exotic applications
 • Use of Proprietary models for benchmarking without distribution
 • Exploration of various computational forms for consequence analysis

 • Takeaways
 • Complex models and results can be easily generated with only data connections to

 • Plug-ins can be stored apart from input decks and re -used as needed

  Decommissioning – Notes for Discussion
 o How can we use knowledge of the debris end state to support 1F3
 Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 retrievals:
 • What do we know from MAAP and MELCOR?
 • What do we know from investigations to date?
 • In-vessel debris mass and location and structures embedded in debris
 • Ex-vessel debris in the pedestal: Stratification? Extent of MCCI?
 • What are our knowledge gaps?
 • What near-term actions/measurements can help gap closure?
 • How does knowledge of debris end state help us to define optimal
 tooling for top-down retrievals of in-vessel debris and sideward
 retrievals of ex-vessel debris?

 5

  Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

  Decommissioning – Notes for Discussion
 o What can we say regarding shield plug removal from 1F3?
 Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 • Drivers for the choice of shield plug removal, and its timing, appear to
 be dose rates and structural integrity – any others?
 • Shield plug removal decreases loads: New shielded platforms should be
 lighter. But some load needs to be added for removal itself.
 • Clearly the rate of debris removal can be faster if shield plugs are
 removed. We can remove RPV upper structures quickly vs debris.
 • What do our models say about fission product (especially Cesium)
 transport through the PCV upper head, leading to high dose rates
 below, within, and above the shield plugs?
 • How do our models compare with measurements?
 • Can we suggest measurements for model improvement?

 6

$1/                                         &

Fauske & Associates Proprietary Class 2 © 2025 Fauske & Associates, a Division of Stone & Webster, LLC. All Rights Reserved

Decommissioning – Notes for Discussion
  o How can we quickly resolve the criticality issue for debris retrieval?
Fauske & Associates, LLC Proprietary Class 2

 • Since the current debris state is not critical, the key assumption must be
 that creating debris pieces with water-filled porosity leads to a
 condition that could be critical – is this true?
 • TMI debris collection did not lead to criticality – why doesn’t this
 eliminate the issue for Fukushima?
 • Where are we worried about criticality – in the PCV or in containers/unit
 cans used for debris collection?
 • What, if any, debris compositions predicted by our models (MAAP,
 MELCOR, MELSPREAD, etc.) can lead to a criticality issue, and how does
 uncertainty in our models affect the issue?
 • Where should we sample to help resolve the issue?

 FRAME Workshop: Insights from
 the Fukushima Daiichi Accident
 and Knowledge Dissemination

 Post-1F High-level actions (international)
 • Post-accident analyses have significantly strengthened accident
 management strategies and informed D&D practices.
 • Enhanced training protocols, including revised training and drills,
 along with deployment of additional on-site and mobile
 emergency equipment, have improved operational preparedness
 for unforeseen events.

$1/                               &



 Remaining knowledge gaps and future needs
 • Despite progress, industry-wide adoption of improved accident analysis
 codes has been limited and remains a challenge.
 • Continued validation and refinement of existing severe accident models,
 especially for bounding and best-estimate scenarios, is necessary.
 • RPV failure models should be improved to increase the accuracy of
 predictions regarding both the mode and timing of failure.
 • Additional testing on molten core-concrete interaction (MCCI) is needed,
 focusing on:
 • corium composition and thermophysical properties;
 • effect of high-metallic debris content;
 • impacts on structural integrity and containment performance.
 • Ongoing monitoring of 1F accident findings is essential to anticipate future
 challenges in severe accident management.
 • Greater deployment of advanced instrumentation and monitoring should be
 pursued for both current and next-generation reactors

 Key takeaways and proposed directions (1/2)
  • Sustained international collaboration is crucial to integrate lessons
 learned from the 1F accident and advance safety for both current and
 next-generation reactors.
  • Launch new collaborative projects targeting key knowledge gaps in:
 • passive safety systems performance;
 • source term behavior and implications for emergency planning zones;
 • materials behavior in advanced non-LWR designs.
  • Improve coordination of TH and SA research to optimize use of new
 and existing experimental infrastructure.

                                             &                                 $1/

 Key takeaways and proposed directions (2/2)
 • Establish a strategic, long-term research roadmap that balances
 diverse stakeholder priorities while securing stable funding and
 engagement.
 • Continue developing and upgrading state
 state-of-the-art
 of the art TH an
 and SA
 analysis codes to support licensing and operation
 oper of both
 conventional and advanced reactor designs.
 • Develop a comprehensive understanding of the risks associated
 with implementing emerging technologies (AI, AM, digital tools..)
 that hold significant transformative potential for enhancing
 nuclear safety, efficiency, and performance.
 • Establish robust, interoperable data ecosystems to support
 advanced analytical methods and ensure reliable, data-driven
 decision-making approaches.

 Outcomes from this meeting..
 • Potential need to update 1F1 analyses assuming some level of
 functionality for the ICs
 • Is industry data available (Dresden, Nine Mile Point, ..) on IC performance
 to support model validation?
 • Updated MAAP-MELCOR cross walk regarding in-core melt
 progression/H2 production….
 • Importance of LH failure modeling/testing…
